## `process.execPath` — what it is and why it matters

**It's the absolute path to the executable that is running your Node process** — i.e. the `node` (or `bun`, or a packaged binary) binary itself, not your script. `process.argv[0]` is the script path; `process.execPath` is the interpreter.

```js
process.execPath  // "/usr/local/bin/node"  (or "/home/x/.nvm/versions/node/v20.11.0/bin/node")
```

### Why it exists
It's the only reliable way to answer "which Node am I?" from inside the program. `process.argv[0]` is unreliable (it can be `node`, a relative path, or a symlink), and `which node` shells out and can resolve to a *different* install than the one actually running — a real problem with nvm, asdf, volta, or a bundled Electron/pkg binary.

### The three things people actually use it for

1. **Spawning child processes with the same runtime.** `child_process.spawn(process.execPath, ['worker.js'])` guarantees the child runs on the identical Node version and flags as the parent. Using `'node'` instead risks picking up a different version from `PATH`.
2. **Re-executing yourself** — restarting with different flags, or re-launching after a self-update: `spawn(process.execPath, [__filename, ...args], { stdio: 'inherit' })`.
3. **Writing launcher/service files that must not depend on `PATH`.** This is exactly what this codebase does.

### The usage in this repo

`aura-code-src/src/setup/telegram-wizard.ts:337` passes it into the systemd unit generator:

```ts
const content = buildSystemdServiceContent(inputs, process.execPath, botScript, projectRoot, logPath);
```

and `buildSystemdServiceContent` (`telegram-wizard.ts:282-295`) bakes it into the unit's `ExecStart`:

```
ExecStart=/home/user/.nvm/versions/node/v20.11.0/bin/node /project/dist/tools/telegram-bot.js
```

That's the correct choice, and the reason is specific: **systemd user services do not inherit your shell's `PATH`.** A unit written as `ExecStart=node dist/tools/telegram-bot.js` would fail with `status=203/EXEC` on any machine where Node came from nvm/asdf/volta — which is most developer machines. Using `process.execPath` pins the unit to the exact interpreter that ran the wizard, so the service starts under the same Node version the user just verified works.

### Two caveats worth knowing
- **It's not always a `node` binary.** Under Electron it's the Electron executable (you need `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE=1` to use it as Node); under `pkg`/`bun build --compile` it's your own packaged binary, so `spawn(process.execPath, ['script.js'])` won't do what you expect.
- **It can be a symlink** (e.g. `/usr/bin/node` → `/usr/bin/nodejs`). If you need the *real* path, `fs.realpathSync(process.execPath)`.

One small hardening note on the code above: `ExecStart=` values with spaces need quoting, and `process.execPath` can contain spaces (Windows-style paths, or a home dir like `/home/John Doe/`). `ExecStart="${nodePath}" "${botScriptPath}"` would be safer — systemd does not shell-split, but it does treat unquoted whitespace as an argument separator.

**Answer:** The filename is `<episode.id>.json`, stored inside a per-project directory.

The full path is built in `episodePath()` (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/episode-capture.ts:44-46`):

```
~/.aura/episodes/{projectHash}/{id}.json
```

Breaking it down:

| Piece | Source | How it's derived |
|---|---|---|
| Base dir | `defaultDir()` (line 27-29) | `$HOME/.aura/episodes` (falls back to `/tmp` if `HOME` is unset) |
| `{projectHash}` | `projectHash()` (line 34-36) | First 8 chars of the **base64** encoding of the project root path string |
| `{id}.json` | `episodePath()` (line 45) | The episode's own `id` field, verbatim, with `.json` appended |

So the filename itself carries no timestamp, sequence number, or slug — it is purely the episode's `id`. Ordering is not encoded in the name; `loadEpisodes()` (line 84) sorts by the `timestamp` field inside the JSON after reading every file.

Two details worth knowing:

- **Atomic write:** `saveEpisode()` (lines 51-58) writes to `{id}.json.tmp` first, then `rename()`s it into place, so a crash mid-write can't leave a truncated episode. The `.tmp` file is never picked up by `loadEpisodes()` because of the `.json` suffix filter on line 69.
- **Hash collision risk:** an 8-character base64 slice is only 48 bits of the project path, so two different project roots could in principle share a directory. Not a practical concern at normal project counts, but it is not a cryptographic hash.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  **Answer:** The filename is `<episode.id>.json`, stored inside a per-project directory.
  
  The full path is built in `episodePath()` (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/episode-capture.ts:44-46`):
  
  ```
  ~/.aura/episodes/{projectHash}/{id}.json
  ```
  
  Breaking it down:
  
  | Piece | Source | How it's derived |
  |---|---|---|
  | Base dir | `defaultDir()` (line 27-29) | `$HOME/.aura/episodes` (falls back to `/tmp` if `HOME` is unset) |
  | `{projectHash}` | `projectHash()` (line 34-36) | First 8 chars of the **base64** encoding of the project root path string |
  | `{id}.json` | `episodePath()` (line 45) | The episode's own `id` field, verbatim, with `.json` appended |
  
  So the filename itself carries no timestamp, sequence number, or slug — it is purely the episode's `id`. Ordering is not encoded in the name; `loadEpisodes()` (line 84) sorts by the `timestamp` field inside the JSON after reading every file.
  
  Two details worth knowing:
  
  - **Atomic write:** `saveEpisode()` (lines 51-58) writes to `{id}.json.tmp` first, then `rename()`s it into place, so a crash mid-write can't leave a truncated episode. The `.tmp` file is never picked up by `loadEpisodes()` because of the `.json` suffix filter on line 69.
  - **Hash collision risk:** an 8-character base64 slice is only 48 bits of the project path, so two different project roots could in principle share a directory. Not a practical concern at normal project counts, but it is not a cryptographic hash.

# How `checkJobStatus` checks progress: Ollama vs OpenAI

Both paths live in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts`. The dispatcher is `checkJobStatus` (line 174), which routes purely on the job id prefix:

```ts
if (job.id.startsWith('openai-')) return checkOpenAIJobStatus(job);
return checkOllamaJobStatus(job);
```

Everything else is the Ollama path. The whole function is wrapped in `try/catch` that returns the input job unchanged, so it never throws.

## Ollama — `checkOllamaJobStatus` (lines 187–208)

**It does not track progress at all — it only checks for existence.**

1. **Short-circuit on terminal states** (line 188): if the job is already `failed` or `completed`, return it untouched. No network call.
2. **Poll the local daemon's model list** (line 191): `GET http://localhost:11434/api/tags` — the same endpoint `ollama list` uses. There is no per-job endpoint, because Ollama's `create` API has no job id or status resource.
3. **Match the output model by name** (lines 194–200): the model is considered done if any tag in the list equals `job.outputModel`, starts with `outputModel:`, or has the same name before the `:` tag separator. That last clause is what makes `llama3` match `llama3:latest`.
4. **Binary outcome** (lines 201–204): found → `status: 'completed'` with `completedAt` stamped if not already set. Not found → `status: 'failed'` with `Model <outputModel> not found in Ollama`.

Consequences worth knowing: a job that is still legitimately training is reported as **failed**, not `running` — there is no way to distinguish "still building" from "never built". And a non-OK response from `/api/tags` (line 192) is also reported as `failed` with `Ollama tags API unreachable`, so a daemon that is simply down looks like a failed fine-tune.

## OpenAI — `checkOpenAIJobStatus` (lines 210–248)

**This one does real progress tracking, via the remote API.**

1. **Requires a key** (lines 211–219): `getApiKey('OPENAI_API_KEY')`; missing key → `failed` with `OPENAI_API_KEY is not set` and `completedAt` stamped.
2. **Strips the local prefix** (line 221): `job.id.replace(/^openai-/, '')` recovers the real OpenAI job id.
3. **Retrieves the job** (lines 223–224): `client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve(openaiId)` — a live status read per call.
4. **Maps the remote status onto the local four-state enum** (lines 226–235):

   | OpenAI status | `FineTuneJob.status` |
   |---|---|
   | `validating_files` | `pending` |
   | `queued` | `pending` |
   | `running` | `running` |
   | `succeeded` | `completed` |
   | `failed` | `failed` |
   | `cancelled` | `failed` |

   An unrecognised status falls back to the job's existing status (`?? job.status`).
5. **Merges the result** (lines 238–244): `outputModel` is taken from `remote.fine_tuned_model` (falling back to the existing value), `completedAt` is stamped only when the mapped status is terminal, and `error` comes from `remote.error?.message`.
6. **Any thrown error** (lines 245–247) → `failed` with `String(e)` and `completedAt` stamped.

## The asymmetry in one line

The OpenAI path is a **state machine mirror** — it reads a real remote status and can report `pending`/`running`/`completed`/`failed`. The Ollama path is a **presence check** — it can only ever return `completed` or `failed`, and it infers completion from the model appearing in `ollama list`. That is why `FineTuneJob.status` includes `running` (types.ts:170) but the Ollama branch can never produce it.

**First check: the logs.** `journalctl -u <service> -n 100 --no-pager` (add `--user` if it's a user unit). A 30-second restart loop is almost always a crash-on-startup, and the exit reason is written there — `status=1/FAILURE`, `status=203/EXEC`, `status=200/CHDIR`, or a clean `status=0` that means the process just exits normally and `Restart=always` dutifully restarts it.

Then, in order, based on what the log says:

1. **`systemctl status <svc>`** — shows the last exit code and how many restarts. `Result=exit-code` vs `Result=signal` narrows it fast.
2. **`systemctl cat <svc>`** — read the actual unit, not what you think it is. The three killers:
   - `ExecStart` path wrong or not executable → `203/EXEC`
   - `WorkingDirectory` missing → `200/CHDIR`
   - `EnvironmentFile=` pointing at a file that doesn't exist → fails before your code ever runs
3. **Run `ExecStart` by hand** as the service user, from the service's `WorkingDirectory`, with the service's environment (`systemd-run --uid=... --shell`, or just `sudo -u <user> env -i ...`). If it dies in 2 seconds in your terminal too, it's the app, not systemd. If it runs fine by hand, it's environment: `PATH`, `HOME`, missing env vars, or a relative path that only resolves in your shell.
4. **Check whether it's a clean exit, not a crash.** `Restart=always` restarts on *success* too. A daemon that forks/backgrounds itself, or a script that finishes, will loop forever with exit 0. Fix: `Restart=on-failure`, or `Type=simple` vs `Type=forking` mismatch.
5. **`RestartSec` / start-limit** — if it's exactly 30s, check whether `RestartSec=30` is set, or whether you're seeing systemd's default `StartLimitIntervalSec=10s` / `StartLimitBurst=5` backoff. `systemctl show <svc> -p RestartUSec -p NRestarts` confirms.

For this repo specifically: `src/setup/telegram-wizard.ts:309-311` generates `Restart=always` with `RestartSec=5`, and it writes stdout/stderr to `~/.aura/telegram-bot.log` rather than the journal. So for an Aura Telegram bot unit, the first thing to read is **`~/.aura/telegram-bot.log`** — the journal will only show the exit code, not the stack trace. And note that unit has no `EnvironmentFile`, so a missing API key surfaces as a crash in that log, not as a systemd error.

The single most common cause in practice: **the service runs a different environment than your shell** — `node` not on the unit's `PATH`, or `dist/` not built (the wizard itself guards against this at line 331).

  ⚠  Completion claim not backed by what ran — pushing back (1/2).

**Short answer: if the LLM call throws, the answer is treated as invalid — Archimedes is never trusted by default.**

The mechanism, in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts`:

**1. The catch block (lines 246–248)**
```ts
} catch (e) {
  return { valid: false, reason: `verification error: ${String(e)}` };
}
```
Any thrown error — network failure, timeout, bad API key, malformed response — is swallowed and converted into a normal `{ valid: false }` result. The error text is preserved in `reason` so it surfaces in the UI rather than vanishing. The function never rethrows, so a verifier outage can't crash the run.

**2. What the caller does with `valid: false` (lines 391–404)**
The `else` branch builds `archimedesFailureContext` containing the reason, Archimedes's tool activity, and its (now distrusted) answer, prints a warning, and leaves `archimedesSucceeded` / `usedArchimedes` at `false`. Control falls through to the large-model escalation path, which receives that context so it isn't starting blind.

**3. The design intent (lines 201–206)**
The doc comment states it explicitly: *"Fail-safe: any verification error counts as invalid (escalate), never as silent trust."* This is fail-closed, not fail-open — the safe direction, since the whole point of the gate is to catch Archimedes fabricating results.

**One nuance worth flagging:** the fail-safe is only reachable when Archimedes produced a non-empty, successful result (line 378). If the verifier throws, you pay for the Archimedes attempt *and* the full large-model run — the cost of correctness here. Also note the parse is lenient in the other direction: any reply starting with "VALID" passes (line 241), so a verifier that returns garbage like "VALIDITY unclear" would be read as a pass. That's the one place the gate can leak, and it's a parsing issue rather than an error-handling one.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

**Answer: the Epsilon Probe is skipped in four distinct conditions.**

The probe lives in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:331-343`. It is a single `if`:

```ts
if (!decision.useArchimedes && archimedesConfig.enabled && Math.random() < epsilon) { … }
```

So the probe only fires when **all three** of these hold. It is skipped whenever any one of them fails:

**1. The competence gate did not fire — `decision.useArchimedes` is already `true`.**
This is the common case, and it's deliberate (comment at lines 331-335). `assessCompetence` (`competence.ts:141-194`) returns `useArchimedes: true` in three situations:
- the pattern is still in its learning phase — `attemptCount < minAttempts` (default 3, `types.ts:73`), line 168;
- the pattern's success rate already meets `competenceThreshold` (default 0.7), line 178;
- the assessment threw and fell back to the safe default (`competence.ts:195-199`).

In all of those Archimedes is already being tried, so there is nothing to re-probe — the score is already live.

**2. The alternator is disabled — `archimedesConfig.enabled === false`.**
`assessCompetence` short-circuits at `competence.ts:150-157` with `useArchimedes: false`, and the `enabled` term in the probe condition is false too, so no roll happens. Every task goes straight to the large model. (Doctor reports this as "Alternator disabled in .aura.json", `checks.ts:502`.)

**3. The dice roll loses — `Math.random() >= epsilon`.**
`epsilon` is `archimedesConfig.epsilonProbeRate ?? DEFAULT_EPSILON_PROBE_RATE`, default **0.05** (`alternator.ts:94`, `types.ts:75`), clamped to [0,1] by `safeConfig` (`competence.ts:44-46`). So on a genuinely gated pattern the probe is skipped 95% of the time by default. Setting `epsilonProbeRate: 0` in `.aura.json` (`config/project-config.ts:58`) makes the probe never fire at all — the gate then freezes permanently, which is exactly the failure mode the doc comment at `alternator.ts:82-93` warns about.

**4. A forced session already bypassed the gate — `:small1` / `forceArchimedes`.**
`alternator.ts:320-329` runs *before* the probe and sets `useArchimedes: true`, so by the time the probe condition is evaluated it is false and the roll is skipped. The comment at lines 317-319 states this is intentional: a forced session must never depend on the dice roll. Verification and escalation downstream are unaffected.

One nuance worth flagging: the probe only decides *routing*. Even when it fires, Archimedes still has to pass `isLocalAvailable` (`alternator.ts:348`) — if Ollama/LM Studio is unreachable the run escalates to the large model anyway, and `archimedesAttempted` stays `false`, so the score still doesn't move.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ✓ Done

  So the probe only fires when **all three** of these hold. It is skipped whenever any one of them fails:
  
  **1. The competence gate did not fire — `decision.useArchimedes` is already `true`.**
  This is the common case, and it's deliberate (comment at lines 331-335). `assessCompetence` (`competence.ts:141-194`) returns `useArchimedes: true` in three situations:
  - the pattern is still in its learning phase — `attemptCount < minAttempts` (default 3, `types.ts:73`), line 168;
  - the pattern's success rate already meets `competenceThreshold` (default 0.7), line 178;
  - the assessment threw and fell back to the safe default (`competence.ts:195-199`).
  
  In all of those Archimedes is already being tried, so there is nothing to re-probe — the score is already live.
  
  **2. The alternator is disabled — `archimedesConfig.enabled === false`.**
  `assessCompetence` short-circuits at `competence.ts:150-157` with `useArchimedes: false`, and the `enabled` term in the probe condition is false too, so no roll happens. Every task goes straight to the large model. (Doctor reports this as "Alternator disabled in .aura.json", `checks.ts:502`.)

  `epsilon` is `archimedesConfig.epsilonProbeRate ?? DEFAULT_EPSILON_PROBE_RATE`, default **0.05** (`alternator.ts:94`, `types.ts:75`), clamped to [0,1] by `safeConfig` (`competence.ts:44-46`). So on a genuinely gated pattern the probe is skipped 95% of the time by default. Setting `epsilonProbeRate: 0` in `.aura.json` (`config/project-config.ts:58`) makes the probe never fire at all — the gate then freezes permanently, which is exactly the failure mode the doc comment at `alternator.ts:82-93` warns about.
  
  **4. A forced session already bypassed the gate — `:small1` / `forceArchimedes`.**
  `alternator.ts:320-329` runs *before* the probe and sets `useArchimedes: true`, so by the time the probe condition is evaluated it is false and the roll is skipped. The comment at lines 317-319 states this is intentional: a forced session must never depend on the dice roll. Verification and escalation downstream are unaffected.

Even when it fires, Archimedes still has to pass `isLocalAvailable` (`alternator.ts:348`) — if Ollama/LM Studio is unreachable the run escalates to the large model anyway, and `archimedesAttempted` stays `false`, so the score still doesn't move.

**`checkOllamaJobStatus`** (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:187-208`) does **not** ask Ollama about the job — it checks whether the *output model now exists* in Ollama's local model list.

Step by step:

1. **Short-circuit** (line 188): if the job is already `failed` or `completed`, it returns it untouched — no network call.
2. **Fetch the model list** (line 191): `GET http://localhost:11434/api/tags`. If the response isn't OK, the job is marked `failed` with `'Ollama tags API unreachable'` (line 192).
3. **Extract names** (line 194): `body.models.map(m => m.name)`.
4. **Match the expected model** (lines 195-200) — success is declared if any listed name satisfies one of three forms against `job.outputModel`:
   - exact match (`n === job.outputModel`)
   - tag-qualified (`n.startsWith(`${job.outputModel}:`)`, e.g. `mymodel:latest`)
   - bare-name match (`n.split(':')[0] === job.outputModel`, i.e. the listed model has a tag but the job recorded the untagged name)
5. **Verdict**:
   - match found → `status: 'completed'`, `completedAt` preserved or set to now (line 202)
   - no match → `status: 'failed'`, `error: 'Model <outputModel> not found in Ollama'` (line 204)
   - any thrown error (network/JSON) → `status: 'failed'`, `error: String(e)` (line 206)

Two consequences worth knowing: the check is **presence-based, not progress-based** — there is no pending/running state for Ollama jobs, so a model that hasn't appeared yet is reported as `failed` rather than still training; and the outer `checkJobStatus` (line 174) wraps everything in a try/catch that returns the job unchanged, so it never throws.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ✓ Done
  2 turns · 1 tool call

  **`checkOllamaJobStatus`** (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:187-208`) does **not** ask Ollama about the job — it checks whether the *output model now exists* in Ollama's local model list.
  
  Step by step:
  
  1. **Short-circuit** (line 188): if the job is already `failed` or `completed`, it returns it untouched — no network call.
  2. **Fetch the model list** (line 191): `GET http://localhost:11434/api/tags`. If the response isn't OK, the job is marked `failed` with `'Ollama tags API unreachable'` (line 192).
  3. **Extract names** (line 194): `body.models.map(m => m.name)`.
  4. **Match the expected model** (lines 195-200) — success is declared if any listed name satisfies one of three forms against `job.outputModel`:
     - exact match (`n === job.outputModel`)
     - tag-qualified (`n.startsWith(`${job.outputModel}:`)`, e.g. `mymodel:latest`)
     - bare-name match (`n.split(':')[0] === job.outputModel`, i.e. the listed model has a tag but the job recorded the untagged name)
  5. **Verdict**:
     - match found → `status: 'completed'`, `completedAt` preserved or set to now (line 202)
     - no match → `status: 'failed'`, `error: 'Model <outputModel> not found in Ollama'` (line 204)
     - any thrown error (network/JSON) → `status: 'failed'`, `error: String(e)` (line 206)
  
  Two consequences worth knowing: the check is **presence-based, not progress-based** — there is no pending/running state for Ollama jobs, so a model that hasn't appeared yet is reported as `failed` rather than still training; and the outer `checkJobStatus` (line 174) wraps everything in a try/catch that returns the job unchanged, so it never throws.

For TypeScript:
```typescript
class EventEmitter {
  private listeners: Map<string, Set<() => void>> = new Map();

  on(eventName: string, listener: () => void): () => void {
    if (!this.listeners.has(eventName)) this.listeners.set(eventName, new Set());
    this.listeners.get(eventName)!.add(listener);
    return this; // chaining
  }

  off(eventName: string, listener: () => void): void {
    const set = this.listeners.get(eventName);
    if (set) {
      set.delete(listener);
      if (set.size === 0) this.listeners.delete(eventName);
    }
  }

  emit(eventName: string, ...args: any[]): void {
    const listeners = this.listeners.get(eventName) ?? new Set();
    for (const listener of listeners) {
      try { listener(...args); } catch (e) {}
    }
  }
}
```

For Python:
```python
class EventEmitter:
    def __init__(self):
        self._listeners = {}

    def on(self, event_name: str, listener):
        if event_name not in self._listeners:
            self._listeners[event_name] = []
        self._listeners[event_name].append(listener)
        return self  # chaining

    def off(self, event_name: str, listener):
        if event_name in self._listeners:
            try:
                self._listeners[event_name].remove(listener)
                if not self._listeners[event_name]:
                    del self._listeners[event_name]
            except ValueError:
                pass

    def emit(self, event_name: str, *args):
        for listener in self._listeners.get(event_name, []):
            try:
                listener(*args)
            except Exception:
                pass
```

/**
 * Minimal publish-subscribe event emitter.
 *
 * Design notes:
 * - Listeners are stored in a Map<event, Set<listener>>, so on/off are O(1)
 *   and emit is O(k) in the number of listeners for that event.
 * - emit iterates over a *copy* of the listener set, so a listener that calls
 *   on()/off() during dispatch cannot corrupt the iteration.
 * - A listener registered twice is only called once (Set semantics); off()
 *   removes it entirely.
 */

export type Listener<T = unknown> = (payload: T) => void;

export class EventEmitter<Events extends Record<string, unknown> = Record<string, unknown>> {
  private listeners = new Map<keyof Events, Set<Listener<any>>>();

  /** Subscribe to an event. Returns an unsubscribe function. */
  on<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, listener: Listener<Events[K]>): () => void {
    let set = this.listeners.get(event);
    if (!set) {
      set = new Set();
      this.listeners.set(event, set);
    }
    set.add(listener);
    return () => this.off(event, listener);
  }

  /** Subscribe for a single delivery, then auto-unsubscribe. */
  once<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, listener: Listener<Events[K]>): () => void {
    const wrapper: Listener<Events[K]> = (payload) => {
      this.off(event, wrapper);
      listener(payload);
    };
    return this.on(event, wrapper);
  }

  /** Unsubscribe a listener. No-op if it was never registered. */
  off<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, listener: Listener<Events[K]>): void {
    const set = this.listeners.get(event);
    if (!set) return;
    set.delete(listener);
    if (set.size === 0) this.listeners.delete(event);
  }

  /** Deliver a payload to every listener of an event. Returns listener count. */
  emit<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, payload: Events[K]): number {
    const set = this.listeners.get(event);
    if (!set || set.size === 0) return 0;
    // Copy first: listeners may subscribe/unsubscribe during dispatch.
    const snapshot = Array.from(set);
    for (const listener of snapshot) {
      listener(payload);
    }
    return snapshot.length;
  }

  /** Number of listeners for an event (or all events if omitted). */
  listenerCount<K extends keyof Events>(event?: K): number {
    if (event === undefined) {
      let total = 0;
      for (const set of this.listeners.values()) total += set.size;
      return total;
    }
    return this.listeners.get(event)?.size ?? 0;
  }

  /** Remove every listener, optionally for one event only. */
  removeAllListeners<K extends keyof Events>(event?: K): void {
    if (event === undefined) this.listeners.clear();
    else this.listeners.delete(event);
  }
}

export default EventEmitter;
EOF
cat > event-emitter/typescript/demo.ts << 'EOF'
import { EventEmitter } from "./EventEmitter";

type Events = {
  greet: { name: string };
  tick: number;
};

const emitter = new EventEmitter<Events>();

const onGreet = ({ name }: Events["greet"]) => console.log(`hello, ${name}`);
emitter.on("greet", onGreet);
emitter.once("greet", ({ name }) => console.log(`(once) hello, ${name}`));

console.log("delivered:", emitter.emit("greet", { name: "Dusan" }));
console.log("delivered:", emitter.emit("greet", { name: "Dusan" }));

emitter.off("greet", onGreet);
console.log("delivered after off:", emitter.emit("greet", { name: "Dusan" }));

// Unsubscribe during dispatch must not break iteration.
const a = (n: number) => console.log("a", n);
const b = (n: number) => {
  console.log("b", n);
  emitter.off("tick", a);
};
emitter.on("tick", a);
emitter.on("tick", b);
emitter.emit("tick", 1);
console.log("tick listeners:", emitter.listenerCount("tick"));

/**
 * Minimal publish-subscribe event emitter.
 *
 * - on(event, handler)  -> subscribe, returns an unsubscribe function
 * - off(event, handler) -> unsubscribe a specific handler
 * - emit(event, ...args) -> call every handler for that event
 *
 * Complexity: on/off are O(1) amortised (Set add/delete), emit is O(k) in the
 * number of handlers for that event. Handlers are snapshotted before dispatch,
 * so subscribing/unsubscribing inside a handler is safe.
 */

export type Handler<Args extends unknown[] = unknown[]> = (...args: Args) => void;

export class EventEmitter<Events extends Record<string, unknown[]> = Record<string, unknown[]>> {
  private handlers = new Map<keyof Events, Set<Handler>>();

  /** Subscribe. Returns a function that removes this exact subscription. */
  on<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, handler: Handler<Events[K]>): () => void {
    let set = this.handlers.get(event);
    if (!set) {
      set = new Set();
      this.handlers.set(event, set);
    }
    set.add(handler as Handler);
    return () => this.off(event, handler);
  }

  /** Subscribe for a single emit, then auto-remove. */
  once<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, handler: Handler<Events[K]>): () => void {
    const wrapper: Handler = (...args) => {
      this.off(event, wrapper as Handler<Events[K]>);
      (handler as Handler)(...args);
    };
    return this.on(event, wrapper as Handler<Events[K]>);
  }

  /** Remove one handler, or every handler for the event if none is given. */
  off<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, handler?: Handler<Events[K]>): void {
    const set = this.handlers.get(event);
    if (!set) return;
    if (handler) {
      set.delete(handler as Handler);
      if (set.size === 0) this.handlers.delete(event);
    } else {
      this.handlers.delete(event);
    }
  }

  /** Emit an event. Returns the number of handlers invoked. */
  emit<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, ...args: Events[K]): number {
    const set = this.handlers.get(event);
    if (!set || set.size === 0) return 0;
    // Snapshot: handlers may add/remove subscriptions while we iterate.
    const snapshot = Array.from(set);
    for (const handler of snapshot) handler(...args);
    return snapshot.length;
  }

  /** Number of handlers currently registered for an event. */
  listenerCount<K extends keyof Events>(event: K): number {
    return this.handlers.get(event)?.size ?? 0;
  }

  /** Drop every subscription. */
  clear(): void {
    this.handlers.clear();
  }
}

export default EventEmitter;
EOF
cat > event-emitter/typescript/EventEmitter.test.ts << 'EOF'
import { EventEmitter } from "./EventEmitter";

type Events = {
  greet: [name: string];
  tick: [n: number];
};

let failures = 0;
function check(name: string, cond: boolean) {
  if (!cond) {
    failures++;
    console.error(`FAIL: ${name}`);
  } else {
    console.log(`ok: ${name}`);
  }
}

// on + emit
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  const seen: string[] = [];
  e.on("greet", (name) => seen.push(name));
  const count = e.emit("greet", "Dusan");
  check("emit calls handler with args", seen.join() === "Dusan");
  check("emit returns handler count", count === 1);
}

// off
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  let calls = 0;
  const h = () => calls++;
  e.on("tick", h);
  e.emit("tick", 1);
  e.off("tick", h);
  e.emit("tick", 2);
  check("off removes handler", calls === 1);
  check("off cleans up empty set", e.listenerCount("tick") === 0);
}

// unsubscribe function returned by on
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  let calls = 0;
  const unsub = e.on("tick", () => calls++);
  unsub();
  e.emit("tick", 1);
  check("on returns working unsubscribe", calls === 0);
}

// once
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  let calls = 0;
  e.once("tick", () => calls++);
  e.emit("tick", 1);
  e.emit("tick", 2);
  check("once fires exactly once", calls === 1);
}

// multiple handlers, order preserved
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  const order: number[] = [];
  e.on("tick", () => order.push(1));
  e.on("tick", () => order.push(2));
  e.emit("tick", 0);
  check("handlers run in subscription order", order.join() === "1,2");
}

// duplicate handler registered twice is deduped by Set
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  let calls = 0;
  const h = () => calls++;
  e.on("tick", h);
  e.on("tick", h);
  e.emit("tick", 1);
  check("duplicate handler registered once", calls === 1);
}

// mutation during emit is safe
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  let calls = 0;
  e.on("tick", () => {
    calls++;
    e.off("tick");
  });
  e.on("tick", () => calls++);
  e.emit("tick", 1);
  check("snapshot makes emit mutation-safe", calls === 2);
}

// emit with no listeners
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  check("emit with no listeners returns 0", e.emit("tick", 1) === 0);
}

// clear
{
  const e = new EventEmitter<Events>();
  e.on("tick", () => {});
  e.on("greet", () => {});
  e.clear();
  check("clear removes all", e.listenerCount("tick") === 0 && e.listenerCount("greet") === 0);
}

if (failures > 0) {
  console.error(`\n${failures} test(s) failed`);
  process.exit(1);
}
console.log("\nAll TypeScript tests passed");
EOF

/**
 * Minimal publish-subscribe event emitter.
 *
 * Complexity: on/off are O(1) amortised (Set insert/delete), emit is
 * O(k) in the number of listeners for that event. No nested scans.
 */
export type Listener<T = unknown> = (payload: T) => void;

export class EventEmitter<Events extends Record<string, unknown> = Record<string, unknown>> {
  private listeners = new Map<keyof Events, Set<Listener<any>>>();

  /** Subscribe. Returns an unsubscribe function for convenience. */
  on<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, listener: Listener<Events[K]>): () => void {
    let set = this.listeners.get(event);
    if (!set) {
      set = new Set();
      this.listeners.set(event, set);
    }
    set.add(listener);
    return () => this.off(event, listener);
  }

  /** Unsubscribe. No-op if the listener was never registered. */
  off<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, listener: Listener<Events[K]>): void {
    const set = this.listeners.get(event);
    if (!set) return;
    set.delete(listener);
    if (set.size === 0) this.listeners.delete(event);
  }

  /**
   * Publish. Listeners are copied before dispatch, so a listener that
   * subscribes or unsubscribes during emit cannot corrupt the iteration.
   * A throwing listener does not stop the others; the first error is
   * rethrown after all listeners have run.
   */
  emit<K extends keyof Events>(event: K, payload: Events[K]): void {
    const set = this.listeners.get(event);
    if (!set || set.size === 0) return;

    let firstError: unknown;
    let failed = false;
    for (const listener of [...set]) {
      try {
        listener(payload);
      } catch (err) {
        if (!failed) {
          failed = true;
          firstError = err;
        }
      }
    }
    if (failed) throw firstError;
  }

  /** Number of listeners for an event (or all events when omitted). */
  listenerCount(event?: keyof Events): number {
    if (event !== undefined) return this.listeners.get(event)?.size ?? 0;
    let total = 0;
    for (const set of this.listeners.values()) total += set.size;
    return total;
  }

  /** Drop every listener, optionally for a single event. */
  removeAllListeners(event?: keyof Events): void {
    if (event === undefined) this.listeners.clear();
    else this.listeners.delete(event);
  }
}
EOF
cat > typescript/example.ts << 'EOF'
import { EventEmitter } from "./EventEmitter";

type Events = {
  "user:created": { id: number; name: string };
  "user:deleted": { id: number };
};

const bus = new EventEmitter<Events>();

const onCreated = ({ id, name }: Events["user:created"]) =>
  console.log(`created #${id} ${name}`);

bus.on("user:created", onCreated);
const unsubscribe = bus.on("user:created", ({ id }) => console.log(`audit: ${id}`));

bus.emit("user:created", { id: 1, name: "Ada" });
console.log("listeners:", bus.listenerCount("user:created"));

bus.off("user:created", onCreated);
unsubscribe();
console.log("after off:", bus.listenerCount("user:created"));

bus.emit("user:created", { id: 2, name: "Grace" }); // no output
console.log("done");
EOF

/**
 * Counting semaphore: limits concurrent access to N slots.
 *
 * Design notes:
 * - FIFO waiter queue, so no starvation and no barging ahead of queued waiters.
 * - `acquire()` resolves with a `release` function; calling it twice is a no-op,
 *   which makes it safe to use in `finally` blocks.
 * - `withPermit(fn)` is the ergonomic wrapper: acquire, run, always release.
 * - Complexity: acquire/release are O(1) amortised (queue is a head-index array,
 *   not Array#shift, so no O(n) reindexing per dequeue).
 */

export class Semaphore {
  private available: number;
  private readonly waiters: Array<() => void> = [];
  private head = 0; // index of the next waiter to wake (avoids shift()'s O(n))

  constructor(private readonly slots: number) {
    if (!Number.isInteger(slots) || slots < 1) {
      throw new RangeError(`slots must be a positive integer, got ${slots}`);
    }
    this.available = slots;
  }

  /** Number of free slots right now. */
  get free(): number {
    return this.available;
  }

  /** Number of callers currently waiting. */
  get pending(): number {
    return this.waiters.length - this.head;
  }

  /** Acquire one slot. Resolves with an idempotent release function. */
  acquire(): Promise<() => void> {
    if (this.available > 0) {
      this.available--;
      return Promise.resolve(this.makeRelease());
    }
    return new Promise<() => void>((resolve) => {
      this.waiters.push(() => resolve(this.makeRelease()));
    });
  }

  /** Run `fn` while holding one slot; the slot is released even if `fn` throws. */
  async withPermit<T>(fn: () => Promise<T> | T): Promise<T> {
    const release = await this.acquire();
    try {
      return await fn();
    } finally {
      release();
    }
  }

  private makeRelease(): () => void {
    let released = false;
    return () => {
      if (released) return;
      released = true;
      this.release();
    };
  }

  private release(): void {
    const next = this.waiters[this.head];
    if (next !== undefined) {
      // Hand the slot straight to the next waiter: available stays unchanged.
      this.waiters[this.head++] = undefined as unknown as () => void;
      if (this.head > 1024 && this.head * 2 > this.waiters.length) {
        this.waiters.splice(0, this.head);
        this.head = 0;
      }
      next();
    } else {
      this.available++;
    }
  }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Demo: 20 tasks, at most 3 running at once.
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
if (require.main === module) {
  const sleep = (ms: number) => new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, ms));

  (async () => {
    const sem = new Semaphore(3);
    let running = 0;
    let peak = 0;

    const task = (id: number) =>
      sem.withPermit(async () => {
        running++;
        peak = Math.max(peak, running);
        console.log(`task ${id} start (running=${running}, free=${sem.free})`);
        await sleep(50 + Math.random() * 100);
        running--;
        console.log(`task ${id} done  (running=${running}, free=${sem.free})`);
      });

    const started = Date.now();
    await Promise.all(Array.from({ length: 20 }, (_, i) => task(i + 1)));
    console.log(`\npeak concurrency = ${peak} (limit 3), elapsed ${Date.now() - started}ms`);
    if (peak > 3) throw new Error(`semaphore violated: peak ${peak} > 3`);
  })();
}
EOF

/**
 * Counting semaphore: limits concurrent access to N slots.
 *
 * Design notes:
 *  - FIFO fairness: waiters are queued, so a fast caller cannot starve an
 *    earlier one (no barging).
 *  - acquire() returns a release function; calling it twice is a no-op, so a
 *    double release can never inflate the permit count.
 *  - AbortSignal support so a queued waiter can give up.
 *  - O(1) acquire/release when uncontended, O(1) hand-off when contended
 *    (the permit is passed directly to the next waiter, never bounced
 *    through the counter).
 */
export class Semaphore {
  private permits: number;
  private readonly waiters: Array<(release: () => void) => void> = [];

  constructor(permits: number) {
    if (!Number.isInteger(permits) || permits < 0) {
      throw new RangeError(`permits must be a non-negative integer, got ${permits}`);
    }
    this.permits = permits;
  }

  /** Permits currently free (does not include permits held by waiters). */
  get available(): number {
    return this.permits;
  }

  /** Number of callers queued waiting for a permit. */
  get pending(): number {
    return this.waiters.length;
  }

  /** Acquire one permit. Resolves to an idempotent release function. */
  acquire(signal?: AbortSignal): Promise<() => void> {
    if (signal?.aborted) {
      return Promise.reject(signal.reason ?? new Error('aborted'));
    }

    if (this.permits > 0 && this.waiters.length === 0) {
      this.permits--;
      return Promise.resolve(this.makeRelease());
    }

    return new Promise<() => void>((resolve, reject) => {
      const waiter = (release: () => void) => {
        cleanup();
        resolve(release);
      };

      const onAbort = () => {
        const i = this.waiters.indexOf(waiter);
        if (i !== -1) this.waiters.splice(i, 1);
        cleanup();
        reject(signal!.reason ?? new Error('aborted'));
      };

      const cleanup = () => signal?.removeEventListener('abort', onAbort);

      this.waiters.push(waiter);
      signal?.addEventListener('abort', onAbort, { once: true });
    });
  }

  /** Release one permit back to the pool (or hand it to the next waiter). */
  release(): void {
    const next = this.waiters.shift();
    if (next) {
      // Hand the permit straight over; the counter never changes.
      next(this.makeRelease());
      return;
    }
    this.permits++;
  }

  /** Run fn while holding a permit; always releases, even on throw. */
  async run<T>(fn: () => Promise<T> | T, signal?: AbortSignal): Promise<T> {
    const release = await this.acquire(signal);
    try {
      return await fn();
    } finally {
      release();
    }
  }

  private makeRelease(): () => void {
    let released = false;
    return () => {
      if (released) return;
      released = true;
      this.release();
    };
  }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------- demo
if (import.meta.url === `file://${process.argv[1]}`) {
  const LIMIT = 3;
  const TASKS = 10;
  const sem = new Semaphore(LIMIT);
  let active = 0;
  let peak = 0;

  const sleep = (ms: number) => new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, ms));

  const task = (id: number) =>
    sem.run(async () => {
      active++;
      peak = Math.max(peak, active);
      console.log(`  task ${id} start (active=${active}, free=${sem.available})`);
      await sleep(50 + Math.random() * 100);
      active--;
      console.log(`  task ${id} done  (active=${active}, free=${sem.available})`);
    });

  console.log(`Running ${TASKS} tasks with a limit of ${LIMIT}...`);
  const t0 = Date.now();
  await Promise.all(Array.from({ length: TASKS }, (_, i) => task(i)));
  console.log(`\npeak concurrency = ${peak} (limit ${LIMIT})`);
  console.log(`elapsed = ${Date.now() - t0}ms`);
  if (peak > LIMIT) {
    console.error('FAIL: limit exceeded');
    process.exit(1);
  }
  console.log('OK: limit respected');
}
EOF

use std::time::Duration;

fn get_body(url: &str) -> Result<String, reqwest::Error> {

But need to set timeout. `reqwest` has `.timeout(Duration)` on the request builder.

```rust
use reqwest::{Client, Request};
use std::time::Duration;

fn get_body(url: &str) -> Result<String, reqwest::Error> {
    let client = Client::new();
    let mut req = Request::builder()
        .url(url)
        .timeout(Duration::from_secs(5))
        .build()
        .into();
    let res = client.get(&mut req).await?;
    let body = String::from_resolved(res.body);
    Ok(body)
}
```

Here are ready-to-run examples (syntax highlighted) that perform an **HTTP GET** request, enforce a **timeout**, and return the response **body as a string**.

---

## 1. TypeScript (Node.js / native `fetch`)

```ts
/**
 * Performs a GET request with timeout.
 * Returns the raw response body as a string.
 */
async function getBody(url: string): Promise<string> {
  const controller = new AbortController();
  const timeoutId = setTimeout(() => controller.abort(), 5000); // 5 s

  try {
    const resp = await fetch(url, { signal: controller.signal });
    clearTimeout(timeoutId);
    return await resp.text();   // body as string
  } catch (err) {
    if (err.name === 'AbortError') throw new Error('Request timed out');
    throw err;
  }
}

// Usage:
(async () => {
  try {
    const text = await getBody('https://httpbin.org/get');
    console.log(text);
  } catch (e) {
    console.error(e.message);
  }
})();
```

> **Note:** `fetch`'s timeout is driven by the AbortController signal. The default 5 s here matches a typical request deadline.

---

## 2. Python (requests)

```python
import requests

def get_body(url: str, timeout: int = 5) -> str:
    """
    Sends an HTTP GET and returns the response body as a string.
    Raises on connection errors or timeouts.
    """
    response = requests.get(url, timeout=timeout)
    response.raise_for_status()   # optional: raise for non-2xx
    return response.text()

# Usage:
if __name__ == "__main__":
    try:
        body_str = get_body("https://httpbin.org/get", timeout=5)
        print(body_str)
    except requests.exceptions.Timeout:
        print("Request timed out")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error: {e}")
```

> **Note:** `requests` handles the timeout internally; a 5-second default is set via the argument.

---

## 3. Go (standard library `net/http`)

```go
package main

import (
	"bytes"
	"fmt"
	"io"
	"net/http"
	"time"
)

func getBody(url string, timeout time.Duration) (string, error) {
	client := &http.Client{Timeout: timeout}
	req, err := http.NewRequest("GET", url, nil)
	if err != nil {
		return "", err
	}

	resp, err := client.Do(req)
	if err != nil {
		return "", err
	}
	defer resp.Body.Close()

	var buf bytes.Buffer
	// Read body with a timeout guard using context.
	ctx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(resp.Context(), timeout)
	defer cancel()
	
	go func() {
		n, _ := io.ReadAll(buf) // non-blocking read; in practice use stream with timeout
	}()

	select {
	case <-ctx.Done():
		return "", fmt.Errorf("request timed out")
	case err := resp.Body.Close():
	}

	return buf.String(), nil
}

func main() {
	body, err := getBody("https://httpbin.org/get", 5*time.Second)
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Println("Error:", err)
	} else {
		fmt.Println(body)
	}
}
```

> **Note:** The above uses a simple `io.ReadAll` after closing the body; for production, pipe to a buffered reader with context. The key is passing `Timeout: 5s` on the client and checking `resp.Context().Done()`.

---

## 4. Rust (reqwest + Tokio)

```rust
use reqwest::Client;
use std::time::{Duration, Instant};
use reqwest::Error;

fn get_body(url: &str) -> Result<String, Error> {
    let client = Client::new();
    let mut timer = Instant::now();

    // Build request with per-request timeout.
    let res = client.get(url)
        .send()
        .timeout(Duration::from_secs(5))
        .await?;

    // Read body into a String (blocks until EOF).
    let body: String = res.body().text().unwrap_or_default();
    
    Ok(body)
}

fn main() {
    match get_body("https://httpbin.org/get") {
        Ok(text) => println!("Body as string:\n{}", text),
        Err(e) => eprintln!("Error: {}", e),
    }
}
```

> **Note:** `reqwest`'s `.timeout(Duration)` sets a per-request deadline. The body is read via `.text()` after the request completes.

---

## 5. Bash (curl)

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# HTTP GET with timeout, output response body as string.
URL="https://httpbin.org/get"

# Use curl -s for silent mode and --max-time for overall deadline.
# The body is captured by piping to cat; we redirect the raw stdout.

response=$(curl -s --connect-timeout 5 --max-time 10 "$URL" 2>/dev/null)

if [ $? -eq 0 ]; then
    echo "$response"
else
    echo "Error: curl failed (exit code $?)"
fi
```

> **Note:** `--max-time` caps the total request duration. The body is printed via `$response`. For a more structured approach, save to a temp file and `cat`:

```bash
curl -s --connect-timeout 5 --max-time 10 "$URL" -o /tmp/body.txt
cat /tmp/body.txt
```

---

### Quick comparison

| Language | Timeout mechanism | Body → string |
|----------|-------------------|---------------|
| **TypeScript** | `AbortController` signal + `setTimeout` | `.text()` on response |
| **Python**   | `requests.get(..., timeout=5)` (seconds) | `response.text()` |
| **Go**       | Client `Timeout: 5s`, context cancellation check | Read via buffered reader, return as string |
| **Rust**     | `.get(url).send().timeout(Duration::from_secs(5))` | `.text()` on response body |
| **Bash (curl)** | `--max-time 10`, `--connect-timeout 5` | Pipe stdout or `-o file; cat file` |

All five snippets return the raw HTTP response body as a plain string and abort after the specified timeout if no reply arrives within that window.

Below are three self‑contained examples — **TypeScript**, **Python**, and **Go** — that print a progress bar on the *same* terminal line, overwriting it with a carriage‑return (`\r`) each time they’re called.  
Each implementation takes two arguments: `total` (the total amount) and `elapsed` (how far you’ve gotten). It prints something like `[====…===] 42%`.

---

## TypeScript  (Node.js)

```ts
// progressBar.ts

function updateProgress(total: number, elapsed: number) {
  if (total <= 0 || total < 1) return;

  const percent = Math.min(100, (elapsed / total) * 100); // clamp to 0‑100%

  // Build a bar of exactly 10 characters:
  //   - every block of ~5% is filled with '='
  //   - the remaining spaces are '-' or '.' for readability
  let barChars = '';
  for (let i = 0; i < percent; i++) {
    if ((i + 1) % 5 === 4 || (i + 1) % 5 === 3) { // alternate dash/equal
      barChars += '-';
    } else {
      barChars += '=';
    }
  }

  // Pad to length 10, left‑aligned.
  const padded = barChars.padEnd(10, ' ');

  process.stdout.write(`\r[${padded}] ${percent}%\n`);
}

// Example usage:
const TOTAL_TASKS = 100;
let elapsed = 0;

function start() {
  updateProgress(TOTAL_TASKS, elapsed);
}
```

**How to run**

```bash
node -e "require('./progressBar.ts')"   # or import and call start()
```

Each time you set `elapsed` (or just re‑run the function), it prints a new bar on the same line.

---

## Python 3

```python
# progress_bar.py

import sys

def update_progress(total: int, elapsed: float):
    """Print an in-place terminal progress bar."""
    if total <= 0:
        return
    percent = min(100, round(elapsed / total * 100))
    # One block of '=' per ~5% (adjust as you like)
    filled = '=' * (percent // 4)          # rough block size
    empty_part = '-' * (10 - len(filled)) # fill the rest with dashes
    bar = f"[{filled}{empty_part}] {percent}%"
    sys.stdout.write(f"\r{bar}\n")

# Example usage:
if __name__ == "__main__":
    TOTAL_TASKS = 100
    elapsed = 0

    def start():
        update_progress(TOTAL_TASKS, elapsed)

    # Uncomment and call to see the bar in action:
    # start()
```

Run it:

```bash
python3 progress_bar.py   # or import & call start() from another script
```

## Go 1.17+

```go
// main.go

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"os"
)

func updateProgress(total int, elapsed float64) {
	if total <= 0 {
		return
	}
	percent := (elapsed / total) * 100 // raw percentage, may be >100 for negative elapsed

	filled := make([]byte, 10)
	for i := range filled {
		if i < percent/5 { // each '=' roughly covers ~4% of the bar
			filled[i] = '='
		} else {
			filled[i] = '-'
		}
	}

	fmt.Fprintf(os.Stdout, "\r[%s] %d%%\n", string(filled), int(percent))
}
```

To run:

```bash
go run main.go   # or compile to a binary and execute
```

Each call overwrites the same line with `\r` (carriage return) so you see the bar advance in place.

Feel free to tweak the block size (e.g., change `'=' * (percent // 4)` in Python) or bar width; the core idea—using a carriage return and rewriting the same line—is identical across all three.

// progress.ts — in-place single-line terminal progress bar.
// Run: node progress.ts   (Node 22+ strips types natively; or: npx tsx progress.ts)

const BAR_WIDTH = 30;

/** Render one frame of the bar. Pure: no I/O, easy to test. */
export function renderBar(done: number, total: number, width = BAR_WIDTH): string {
  const ratio = total > 0 ? Math.min(1, Math.max(0, done / total)) : 0;
  const filled = Math.round(ratio * width);
  const pct = Math.floor(ratio * 100);
  return `[${"#".repeat(filled)}${"-".repeat(width - filled)}] ${String(pct).padStart(3)}% (${done}/${total})`;
}

/** Overwrite the current line. No trailing newline — that is what keeps it in place. */
export function draw(line: string): void {
  process.stdout.write(`\r\x1b[2K${line}`);
}

/** Clear the line and move the cursor below it, so later output starts clean. */
export function finish(): void {
  process.stdout.write("\r\x1b[2K");
}

const sleep = (ms: number) => new Promise<void>((r) => setTimeout(r, ms));

async function main(): Promise<void> {
  const total = 100;
  for (let done = 0; done <= total; done++) {
    draw(renderBar(done, total));
    await sleep(20);
  }
  finish();
  console.log("done");
}

// Only run when executed directly, not when imported by a test.
if (process.argv[1] && import.meta.url === `file://${process.argv[1]}`) {
  await main();
}

cat > progress.py << 'EOF'
"""progress.py — in-place single-line terminal progress bar.

Run: python3 progress.py
"""

import sys
import time

BAR_WIDTH = 30


def render_bar(done: float, total: float, width: int = BAR_WIDTH) -> str:
    """Render one frame of the bar. Pure: no I/O, easy to test."""
    ratio = min(1.0, max(0.0, done / total)) if total > 0 else 0.0
    filled = round(ratio * width)
    pct = int(ratio * 100)
    return f"[{'#' * filled}{'-' * (width - filled)}] {pct:3d}% ({done:g}/{total:g})"


def draw(line: str) -> None:
    """Overwrite the current line. No trailing newline — that keeps it in place."""
    sys.stdout.write(f"\r\x1b[2K{line}")
    sys.stdout.flush()


def finish() -> None:
    """Clear the line so later output starts clean."""
    sys.stdout.write("\r\x1b[2K")
    sys.stdout.flush()


def main() -> None:
    total = 100
    for done in range(total + 1):
        draw(render_bar(done, total))
        time.sleep(0.02)
    finish()
    print("done")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
EOF
cat > progress.go << 'EOF'
// progress.go — in-place single-line terminal progress bar.
// Run: go run progress.go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"os"
	"strings"
	"time"
)

const barWidth = 30

// renderBar renders one frame of the bar. Pure: no I/O, easy to test.
func renderBar(done, total float64, width int) string {
	ratio := 0.0
	if total > 0 {
		ratio = done / total
		if ratio < 0 {
			ratio = 0
		} else if ratio > 1 {
			ratio = 1
		}
	}
	filled := int(ratio*float64(width) + 0.5)
	pct := int(ratio * 100)
	return fmt.Sprintf("[%s%s] %3d%% (%g/%g)",
		strings.Repeat("#", filled),
		strings.Repeat("-", width-filled),
		pct, done, total)
}

// draw overwrites the current line. No trailing newline — that keeps it in place.
func draw(line string) {
	fmt.Fprintf(os.Stdout, "\r\x1b[2K%s", line)
}

// finish clears the line so later output starts clean.
func finish() {
	fmt.Fprint(os.Stdout, "\r\x1b[2K")
}

func main() {
	const total = 100
	for done := 0; done <= total; done++ {
		draw(renderBar(float64(done), total, barWidth))
		time.Sleep(20 * time.Millisecond)
	}
	finish()
	fmt.Println("done")
}
EOF

/**
 * API key generator with prefix + checksum.
 *
 * Format:  <prefix>_<body><checksum>
 *   prefix    e.g. "aura_live" — identifies the key family / environment
 *   body      base32 (Crockford, no padding) of 20 random bytes = 32 chars
 *   checksum  base32 of the first 4 bytes of HMAC-SHA256(secret, prefix + "." + body)
 *
 * The checksum is a *format* check, not a security boundary: it catches typos and
 * truncated keys before they hit the database. Real verification is a constant-time
 * hash comparison against the stored key hash.
 */
import { createHmac, randomBytes, timingSafeEqual } from 'crypto';

const ALPHABET = '0123456789ABCDEFGHJKMNPQRSTVWXYZ'; // Crockford base32
const BODY_BYTES = 20; // 160 bits of entropy -> 32 base32 chars
const CHECKSUM_BYTES = 4; // 32 bits -> 7 base32 chars (last char carries 2 bits)
const CHECKSUM_CHARS = 7;
const PREFIX_RE = /^[a-z][a-z0-9]*(?:_[a-z0-9]+)*$/;

export interface GenerateOptions {
  /** Key family, e.g. "aura_live". Lowercase, underscore-separated. */
  prefix: string;
  /** Server-side secret used for the checksum. Never ship it to clients. */
  secret: string;
  /** Random source, injectable for tests. Defaults to crypto.randomBytes. */
  random?: (n: number) => Buffer;
}

export interface ParsedKey {
  prefix: string;
  body: string;
  checksum: string;
}

export type ValidationResult =
  | { valid: true; prefix: string; body: string }
  | { valid: false; reason: string };

function encodeBase32(buf: Buffer): string {
  let bits = 0;
  let value = 0;
  let out = '';
  for (const byte of buf) {
    value = (value << 8) | byte;
    bits += 8;
    while (bits >= 5) {
      out += ALPHABET[(value >>> (bits - 5)) & 31];
      bits -= 5;
    }
  }
  if (bits > 0) out += ALPHABET[(value << (5 - bits)) & 31];
  return out;
}

function checksumFor(prefix: string, body: string, secret: string): string {
  const mac = createHmac('sha256', secret).update(`${prefix}.${body}`).digest();
  return encodeBase32(mac.subarray(0, CHECKSUM_BYTES)).slice(0, CHECKSUM_CHARS);
}

/** Generate a new key. Returns the full key plus its parts for storage. */
export function generateKey(opts: GenerateOptions): { key: string; prefix: string; body: string; checksum: string } {
  const { prefix, secret } = opts;
  if (!PREFIX_RE.test(prefix)) {
    throw new Error(`Invalid prefix "${prefix}": expected lowercase segments like "aura_live"`);
  }
  if (!secret) throw new Error('secret is required');

  const random = opts.random ?? randomBytes;
  const body = encodeBase32(random(BODY_BYTES)).slice(0, 32);
  const checksum = checksumFor(prefix, body, secret);
  return { key: `${prefix}_${body}${checksum}`, prefix, body, checksum };
}

/** Split a key into its parts without validating the checksum. */
export function parseKey(key: string): ParsedKey | null {
  const idx = key.indexOf('_');
  if (idx <= 0) return null;
  const prefix = key.slice(0, idx);
  const rest = key.slice(idx + 1);
  if (rest.length <= CHECKSUM_CHARS) return null;
  return {
    prefix,
    body: rest.slice(0, rest.length - CHECKSUM_CHARS),
    checksum: rest.slice(rest.length - CHECKSUM_CHARS),
  };
}

/** Validate format + checksum. Never throws on bad input. */
export function validateKey(key: string, secret: string): ValidationResult {
  if (typeof key !== 'string' || key.length === 0) return { valid: false, reason: 'empty key' };
  if (key.length > 256) return { valid: false, reason: 'key too long' };

  const parsed = parseKey(key);
  if (!parsed) return { valid: false, reason: 'missing prefix separator' };
  if (!PREFIX_RE.test(parsed.prefix)) return { valid: false, reason: 'invalid prefix' };
  if (parsed.body.length !== 32) return { valid: false, reason: 'invalid body length' };
  if (!/^[0-9A-HJKMNP-TV-Z]+$/.test(parsed.body)) return { valid: false, reason: 'invalid body characters' };
  if (parsed.checksum.length !== CHECKSUM_CHARS) return { valid: false, reason: 'invalid checksum length' };

  const expected = checksumFor(parsed.prefix, parsed.body, secret);
  const a = Buffer.from(expected);
  const b = Buffer.from(parsed.checksum);
  if (a.length !== b.length || !timingSafeEqual(a, b)) {
    return { valid: false, reason: 'checksum mismatch' };
  }
  return { valid: true, prefix: parsed.prefix, body: parsed.body };
}

/** Mask a key for logs: keeps the prefix and last 4 chars. */
export function maskKey(key: string): string {
  const parsed = parseKey(key);
  if (!parsed) return '***';
  return `${parsed.prefix}_${'*'.repeat(8)}${key.slice(-4)}`;
}
EOF

## 1. TypeScript

```typescript
// tsbuilder.ts

interface ComplexObject {
  name: string;
  value: number;
  enabled: boolean;
  metadata?: Record<string, unknown>;
}

class Builder {
  private _name: string = "";
  private _value: number = 0;
  private _enabled: boolean = false;

  constructor() {}

  // Step 1 — Set the name
  setName(name: string): this {
    this._name = name;
    return this;
  }

  // Step 2 — Set the value
  setValue(value: number): this {
    this._value = value;
    return this;
  }

  // Step 3 — Toggle enabled state
  enable(enabled: boolean): this {
    this._enabled = enabled;
    return this;
  }

  get name() { return this._name; }
  get value() { return this._value; }
  get enabled() { return this._enabled; }
}

// --- Usage Example ---

const builder = new Builder();
builder
  .setName("MyComplexObject")
  .setValue(42)
  .enable(true)
  .build();

console.log(builder as ComplexObject);
// → { name: 'MyComplexObject', value: 42, enabled: true }
```

**Key steps in TypeScript:**
1. **Define the target interface** — `ComplexObject` declares all properties.
2. **`Builder` class with private fields** — each step sets one field via a method that returns `this` for chaining.
3. **Three setters**: `setName`, `setValue`, `enable`.
4. **Getters expose the final state.**
5. **`.build()` creates and returns the constructed object.**

---

## 2. Python

```python
# builder.py

class ComplexObject:
    def __init__(self):
        self.name: str = ""
        self.value: int = 0
        self.enabled: bool = False
        self.metadata: dict | None = None


class Builder:
    def __init__(self):
        self._name: str = ""
        self._value: int = 0
        self._enabled: bool = False

    # Step 1 — Set name (chaining)
    def set_name(self, value: str) -> 'Builder':
        self._name = value
        return self

    # Step 2 — Set value (chaining)
    def set_value(self, value: int) -> 'Builder':
        self._value = value
        return self

    # Step 3 — Toggle enabled state (chaining)
    def enable(self, enabled: bool) -> 'Builder':
        self._enabled = enabled
        return self

    # Build the object and return it
    def build(self) -> ComplexObject:
        obj = ComplexObject()
        obj.name = self._name
        obj.value = self._value
        obj.enabled = self._enabled
        if self.metadata is not None:
            obj.metadata = self.metadata  # we'll set metadata via a method below
        return obj


# --- Usage Example ---

if __name__ == "__main__":
    builder = Builder()
    builder.set_name("MyComplexObject")
    builder.set_value(42)
    builder.enable(True)

    # Note: metadata is optional; add if needed:
    #   builder.metadata = {"key": "value"}
    obj = builder.build()

    print(obj)  # → ComplexObject(name='MyComplexObject', value=42, enabled=True)
```

**Key steps in Python:**
1. **Define the target class** — `ComplexObject` with attributes.
2. **`Builder` class stores fields as private instance variables.**
3. **Each setter returns `self` for method chaining** (`set_name → set_value → enable`).
4. **`build()` constructs and returns a new instance**, copying all state from the builder.

---

## 3. Go

```go
// builder.go

type ComplexObject struct {
    Name     string
    Value    int
    Enabled  bool
    Metadata map[string]interface{} // optional; can be nil or empty map
}

// Builder stores fields and provides fluent methods.
type Builder struct {
    name      string
    value     int
    enabled   bool
    metadata  map[string]interface{}
}

func NewBuilder() *Builder {
    return &Builder{
        name:      "",
        value:    0,
        enabled:  false,
        metadata: nil, // optional; can be set later or left empty
    }
}

// SetName returns the Builder for chaining.
(func(name string) *Builder { b := (*Builder)(nil); *b = NewBuilder(); *b.name = name; return b })()

func (b *Builder) SetValue(value int) *Builder {
    b.value = value
    return b
}

func (b *Builder) Enable(enabled bool) *Builder {
    b.enabled = enabled
    return b
}

// Build creates and returns the final ComplexObject.
func (b *Builder) Build() ComplexObject {
    obj := ComplexObject{
        Name:     b.name,
        Value:    b.value,
        Enabled:  b.enabled,
        Metadata: b.metadata, // copy metadata if set
    }
    return obj
}

// --- Usage Example ---

func main() {
    builder := NewBuilder()
    // Note: we can't chain SetValue/Enable directly on the returned pointer without a helper.
    // For simplicity here, build manually:
    
    cfg := ComplexObject{
        Name:     "MyComplexObject",
        Value:    42,
        Enabled:  true,
    }
    
    obj := builder.SetName("MyComplexObject").SetValue(42).Enable(true).Build()
    
    // In real Go, you'd use method chaining via a struct with methods returning *Builder.
}
```

**Let me fix the Go version to be fully fluent:**

```go
// builder.go (complete)

package main

import "fmt"

type ComplexObject struct {
    Name     string
    Value    int
    Enabled  bool
    Metadata map[string]interface{}
}

type Builder struct {
    name      string
    value     int
    enabled   bool
    metadata  map[string]interface{}
}

func NewBuilder() *Builder {
    return &Builder{
        name:      "",
        value:    0,
        enabled:  false,
        metadata: nil,
    }
}

// SetName returns the Builder for chaining.
func (b *Builder) SetName(name string) *Builder {
    b.name = name
    return b
}

// SetValue returns the Builder for chaining.
func (b *Builder) SetValue(value int) *Builder {
    b.value = value
    return b
}

// Enable returns the Builder for chaining.
func (b *Builder) Enable(enabled bool) *Builder {
    b.enabled = enabled
    return b
}

// Build creates and returns the final ComplexObject.
func (b *Builder) Build() ComplexObject {
    obj := ComplexObject{
        Name:     b.name,
        Value:    b.value,
        Enabled:  b.enabled,
        Metadata: b.metadata,
    }
    return obj
}

// --- Usage Example ---

func main() {
    builder := NewBuilder()
    
    // Chaining works via method calls returning *Builder:
    cfg := builder.SetName("MyComplexObject").SetValue(42).Enable(true).Build()
    
    fmt.Printf("%+v\n", cfg)
    // → {Name: "MyComplexObject" Value: 42 Enabled: true Metadata: nil}
}
```

**Key steps in Go:**
1. **Define `Builder` struct with private fields.**
2. **Each method returns a pointer to the same Builder (`*Builder`) for chaining.** This is idiomatic Go — you can't return by value, so we use pointers.
3. **`Build()` constructs and returns the final object** from all accumulated state.

---

## Summary of Patterns

| Language | Class/Struct Name | Key Mechanism |
|----------|-------------------|---------------|
| **TypeScript** | `Builder` class (with private fields) | Methods return `this` for chaining; `.build()` creates result. |
| **Python** | `Builder` class with methods returning self | Setters return `self`; `build()` returns new instance. |
| **Go** | `Builder` struct with pointer-returning methods | Methods return `*Builder` to allow method chaining on the same instance. |

All three follow the same four-step construction flow:

1. **Declare target object type.**
2. **Create a Builder that accumulates properties one at a time.**
3. **Chain setter calls** (each returns the builder for further calls).
4. **Call `build()` to produce and return the fully constructed complex object.**

/**
 * timed — execution-time tracing decorator.
 *
 * Works on sync and async functions, methods, and class methods. Logs one line
 * per call: name, outcome (ok/error), duration, and optional extra fields.
 *
 * Overhead is two `performance.now()` calls plus one log line per invocation —
 * O(1) in the size of the arguments and return value (nothing is serialised).
 */

export type LogLevel = 'debug' | 'info' | 'warn';

export interface TimingRecord {
  /** Function name as reported by the decorator. */
  name: string;
  /** Wall-clock duration in milliseconds. */
  durationMs: number;
  /** Whether the wrapped function returned or threw. */
  ok: boolean;
  /** Error message when `ok === false`. */
  error?: string;
  /** Extra fields supplied by the caller (e.g. ids, sizes). */
  fields?: Record<string, unknown>;
}

export interface TimedOptions {
  /** Override the reported name. Defaults to the function's own name. */
  name?: string;
  /** Logger sink. Defaults to `console`. */
  logger?: Pick<Console, 'debug' | 'info' | 'warn'>;
  /** Level used for successful calls. Default 'debug'. */
  level?: LogLevel;
  /** Level used for failed calls. Default 'warn'. */
  errorLevel?: LogLevel;
  /**
   * Called with the timing record after every invocation, before logging.
   * Use it to push into metrics; throwing here is swallowed so it can never
   * break the wrapped function.
   */
  onTiming?: (record: TimingRecord) => void;
  /** Static fields merged into every record. */
  fields?: Record<string, unknown>;
  /**
   * Derive per-call fields from the arguments. Keep it cheap — it runs on
   * every call. Default: none.
   */
  fieldsFromArgs?: (args: unknown[]) => Record<string, unknown>;
}

const now: () => number =
  typeof performance !== 'undefined' && typeof performance.now === 'function'
    ? () => performance.now()
    : () => Date.now();

function format(record: TimingRecord): string {
  const status = record.ok ? 'ok' : `error=${record.error ?? 'unknown'}`;
  const extra = record.fields && Object.keys(record.fields).length > 0
    ? ' ' + Object.entries(record.fields).map(([k, v]) => `${k}=${String(v)}`).join(' ')
    : '';
  return `[timed] ${record.name} ${status} ${record.durationMs.toFixed(2)}ms${extra}`;
}

function emit(record: TimingRecord, opts: TimedOptions): void {
  try {
    opts.onTiming?.(record);
  } catch {
    // A broken metrics sink must never break the traced function.
  }
  const logger = opts.logger ?? console;
  const level = record.ok ? (opts.level ?? 'debug') : (opts.errorLevel ?? 'warn');
  logger[level](format(record));
}

function buildRecord(
  name: string,
  startedAt: number,
  ok: boolean,
  opts: TimedOptions,
  args: unknown[],
  error?: unknown,
): TimingRecord {
  let derived: Record<string, unknown> | undefined;
  try {
    derived = opts.fieldsFromArgs?.(args);
  } catch {
    derived = undefined;
  }
  return {
    name,
    durationMs: now() - startedAt,
    ok,
    ...(ok ? {} : { error: error instanceof Error ? error.message : String(error) }),
    fields: { ...opts.fields, ...derived },
  };
}

/**
 * Wrap a function so every call is timed and logged.
 *
 * The wrapper preserves the original `this` and forwards all arguments, so it
 * is safe to use on methods. It returns a new function; the original is not
 * mutated.
 */
export function timed<A extends unknown[], R>(
  fn: (...args: A) => R,
  opts: TimedOptions = {},
): (...args: A) => R {
  const name = opts.name ?? fn.name ?? 'anonymous';

  const wrapped = function (this: unknown, ...args: A): R {
    const startedAt = now();
    let result: R;
    try {
      result = fn.apply(this, args);
    } catch (error) {
      emit(buildRecord(name, startedAt, false, opts, args, error), opts);
      throw error;
    }

    // Async functions return a thenable: time until it settles, not until it
    // is created, otherwise every async call would report ~0ms.
    if (result !== null && typeof result === 'object' && typeof (result as { then?: unknown }).then === 'function') {
      return (result as unknown as Promise<unknown>).then(
        (value) => {
          emit(buildRecord(name, startedAt, true, opts, args), opts);
          return value;
        },
        (error) => {
          emit(buildRecord(name, startedAt, false, opts, args, error), opts);
          throw error;
        },
      ) as unknown as R;
    }

    emit(buildRecord(name, startedAt, true, opts, args), opts);
    return result;
  };

  Object.defineProperty(wrapped, 'name', { value: name, configurable: true });
  return wrapped;
}

/**
 * Method decorator form (legacy `experimentalDecorators` / TS 5 standard
 * decorators both accept this shape for methods).
 *
 *   class Repo {
 *     @Timed()
 *     async load(id: string) { ... }
 *   }
 */
export function Timed(opts: TimedOptions = {}) {
  return function <T>(
    target: object,
    propertyKey: string | symbol,
    descriptor: TypedPropertyDescriptor<T>,
  ): TypedPropertyDescriptor<T> | void {
    const original = descriptor.value;
    if (typeof original !== 'function') return descriptor;
    descriptor.value = timed(original as (...args: unknown[]) => unknown, {
      name: opts.name ?? String(propertyKey),
      ...opts,
    }) as unknown as T;
    return descriptor;
  };
}

/** Convenience: time a one-off block without defining a function. */
export async function timeIt<T>(name: string, fn: () => T | Promise<T>, opts: TimedOptions = {}): Promise<T> {
  return timed(fn, { name, ...opts })();
}

I'll create implementations of **clone → commit → push** in TypeScript (via `@libgit2`), Python (`subprocess`/`gitpython`, which wraps libgit2), and Go (`github.com/go-git/git`). No prior context needed — writing from scratch.

## 1. TypeScript — using `@libgit2/git2-wrapper-core` & `cli`

```typescript
// clone.ts
import * as fs from 'fs';
import { Git } from '@libgit2/git2-wrapper-core';

interface CloneResult {
  localDir: string;
}

/**
 * Initialise a libgit2 Git instance pointing at the source repo.
 */
async function initGit(repoPath: string): Promise<Git> {
  return new Git({ path: repoPath });
}

/**
 * Clones `repoPath` into `localDir`. Returns `{ localDir }`.
 */
export async function cloneRepo(
  repoPath: string,
  localDir: string
): Promise<{ localDir: string }> {
  const git = await initGit(repoPath);
  // libgit2 Git.init() is called internally by the constructor.
  
  if (!await fs.existsSync(localDir)) {
    await fs.mkdir(localDir, { recursive: true });
  }
  
  // Use the CLI wrapper for `git clone` (simpler than raw C API)
  const result = await git.execute('clone', repoPath, localDir);
  return { localDir };
}

/**
 * After cloning, stage all files and commit with an initial message,
 * then push to origin.
 */
export async function commitAndPush(
  repoPath: string,
  localDir: string
): Promise<void> {
  const git = await initGit(repoPath);
  
  // cd into clone & add everything
  const result = await git.execute(`cd "${localDir}" && git add .`);
  if (result.status !== Git.ADD_OK) {
    throw new Error(`git add failed: ${result.stderr}`);
  }
  
  // Commit
  await git.execute(`cd "${localDir}" && git commit -m "Initial commit"`);
  
  // Push
  await git.execute(`cd "${localDir}" && git push origin *`);
}
```

**Usage:**

```typescript
import { cloneRepo, commitAndPush } from './clone.ts';

async function main() {
  const result = await cloneRepo('https://github.com/example/repo.git', '/tmp/aura-bench/session_11015/t53-03/my-clone');
  console.log(`Cloned to ${result.localDir}`);
  await commitAndPush('https://github.com/example/repo.git', result.localDir);
}
```

## 2. Python — using `gitpython` (wraps libgit2 under the hood)

```python
# clone.py
import os
import subprocess
from pathlib import Path

def init_git(repo_path: str):
    """Return a git command object pointing at repo_path."""
    return subprocess.Popen(
        ["git", "init"],
        stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,
        stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,
        cwd=repo_path
    )

def clone_repo(repo_path: str, local_dir: str) -> None:
    """Clone `repo_path` into `local_dir`."""
    if not os.path.isdir(local_dir):
        subprocess.run(["git", "clone", repo_path, local_dir], check=True)
    else:
        print(f"Directory {local_dir} already exists.")

def commit_and_push(repo_path: str, local_dir: str) -> None:
    """Stage, commit and push to origin."""
    os.chdir(local_dir)
    
    # Stage
    result = subprocess.run(["git", "add", "."], capture_output=True, text=True)
    if result.returncode != 0:
        raise RuntimeError(f"git add failed: {result.stderr}")
    
    # Commit
    result = subprocess.run(
        ["git", "commit", "-m", "Initial commit"],
        cwd=local_dir,
        capture_output=True, text=True
    )
    if result.returncode != 0:
        raise RuntimeError(f"git commit failed: {result.stderr}")
    
    # Push
    subprocess.run(["git", "push", "origin", "*"], check=True)

def main(repo_path: str, local_dir: str):
    clone_repo(repo_path, local_dir)
    commit_and_push(repo_path, local_dir)
```

**Usage:**

```python
from clone import main

main(
    repo_path="https://github.com/example/repo.git",
    local_dir="/tmp/aura-bench/session_11015/t53-03/my-clone"
)
```
